on selections.
Nodes(Below
Target Step)
Associated with Target Node configuration.
Exception Han-
dler
If conditions for starting the workflow are not met, the
Exception Handler will be performed for the workflow.
• Task Pending: The workflow will wait until all con-
ditions are met before executing.
• Prompt Error: The workflow will fail to start and
an error message will pop up on the PAD.
Accounts Determine which users can view and trigger the but-
ton on the PDA. Admins cannot trigger custom but-
tons.
Enabling/Disabling a Custom Carry button
After creating the custom carry button, its initial status is Disabled and it
will not be shown on the PDA.
Use the enable/disable action to switch the button status.
Editing a Custom Carry button
Ensure the button status is Disabled and use the edit button to modify the
configuration. If the status is Enabled, the Edit action is replaced with the
Detail action, allowing users to only view the configuration without editing.
Deleting a Custom Carry button
Ensure the button status is Disabled and click the delete button to remove
the configuration.

5.15.2 Multiple-step Carry
Description
Multi-Point Carry is used to create a simple carry workflow containing mul-
tiple nodes, which can be triggered as a custom button on the PDA.
Configuration
Click on + to add a configuration.
Fig. 5-649: Multiple-step carry configuration
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
608/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 609 ===
Parameter Description
Config Name Button name to display on the PDA.
Button name must be unique in custom buttons.
Start Step Type The type of the start node in the workflow:
• Map Zone: Area Node in the workflow.
• Node: Layout Node in the workflow.
Target Step
Type
The following nodes of the workflow are as follows.
The workflow will lift up at the start node first and
then go to the following nodes in sequence, lowering
down at the last node:
• Single Node: A single Layout Node in the work-
flow.
• Multiple Nodes: The following nodes are multiple
Layout Nodes.
• Single Map Zone: The target node is a single
Map Zone.
• Multiple Map Zones: The following nodes are mul-
tiple Map Zones.
Release Type To decide which way to release the workflow each
time the robot arrives at a node in the workflow, you
have two options:
• Manual release: Each time the robot reaches a
node, it will wait until the user manually releases
the workflow using a device or PDA.
• Auto release: Each time the robot reaches a
node, it will wait for a configured amount of time
(specified in the Auto release time) and then au-
tomatically release the workflow.
Final Step Decide where to lower the container after the AMR
arrives at the last node:
• Return to Start Step: The AMR will return to the
start node and lower the container.
• Lower Container at End Step: The AMR will lower
the container at the last node.
Accounts Determine which users can view and trigger the but-
ton on the PDA. Admins cannot trigger custom but-
tons.
Enabling/Disabling a Multi-Point Carry button
After creating the Multi-Point Carry button, its initial status is Disabled and
it will not be shown on the PDA.
Use the enable/disable action to switch the button status.
Editing a Multi-Point Carry button
Ensure the button status is Disabled and use the edit button to modify the
configuration. If the status is Enabled, the Edit action is replaced with the
Detail action, allowing users to only view the configuration without editing.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 609/719

move the configuration.

5.16 Standby mode and Wake-up Service
Setting a vehicle to standby
To set a vehicle to standby, you must execute StandByCommand via an
Action Executor Client.
(>>> 6.3 "Action Executors" Page 639 )
Wake-up command
The Wake-up command is the command in charge of waking up vehicles
from standby.
A vehicle in standby mode cannot be commanded. To be able to com-
mand it again, it must be woken up.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 619/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 620 ===
Precondition
• Vehicle is in standby, not (!) switched off.
If the vehicle is switched off, communication with the battery management
system is not possible. The wake-up command will fail with a timeout.
Action Executor interface
WakeUpCommand can be handled by an Action Executor service on
port 50220.
The command is also available in the Robot Manager.
message WakeUpCommand 
{
    // Identify which robot should wake up by its uuid or ip address / port
    oneof robot_identifier
  {
    // The uuid of the robot which should be woken up.
    string robot_uuid = 1;
    // ip address and port of the robot's battery management system
    kuka.common.v1alpha1.HostPort bms_address = 2;
  }
  // optional.            
  // timeout in seconds
  google.protobuf.Duration timeout = 3;
}
CLI examples
Example 1
This command can be used if the IP addresses of the vehicle (or the bat-
tery management system) and of the port are known:
kuka action WakeUpCommand --bms_address 172.31.1.104 --bms_address.port 5000
The Action Executor will respond with the FINISHED state after a few sec-
onds.
A timeout takes a little longer, approx. 30 seconds.
If the battery management system can handle the command, no error is
returned. Otherwise, an error message is returned.
Example 2
kuka action WakeUpCommand --robot_uuid Robot1
The same applies as for the previous example.
In addition: The vehicle must have logged on to the server at least once
and must have transmitted network connection data.
If these preconditions are not met, an error message will be returned, stat-
ing that the vehicle has never transmitted its IP address.
Example 3
Both variants can also be commanded with a timeout:
kuka action WakeUpCommand --robot_uuid Robot1 --timeout.seconds 300
If the gRPC call is successful (i.e. the battery management system is
reached), the action will be in the EXECUTING state. It will remain so un-
til the vehicle is awake or until the specified time expires.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
620/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 621 ===
It takes approx. 60 to 180 seconds for a vehicle to wake up. If the
specified timeout is too small, the command will result in an error. Note:
The vehicle will wake up nevertheless.
5.17 SECS Service (Communicating with a factory host)
General
The SECS Service makes it possible to communicate with a factory host
via a SECS communication link. The factory host is the system that com-
municates with all the devices.
The SECS Service is responsible for:
• Processing of various stream functions
• Maintenance of the relevant variables
• Returning event reports for dedicated event occurrences to the factory
host
The SECS Service supports the SEMI E82 standard. It can process re-
mote commands via SEMI E30 standard stream functions S2,F41 and
S2,F49.
Configuration files
File Description
config/secs/secs-config.yml Configuration of the service
config/secs/libsecs.conf Configuration of the SECS library
5.17.1 Command handling
Standard remote commands
• TRANSFER
• ABORT
• CANCEL
• PAUSE
• RESUME
Custom remote commands
In addition to the standard commands, the SEMI E82 standard allows cus-
tom commands.
The SECS Service's configuration file maps the remote commands (e.g.
PAUSE) to their target Action Executors (e.g. orderengine:50200).
The user can add custom remote commands to the list.
Commands that are not listed will be “mapped” to the default Action Exec-
utor, if present. Otherwise, the command will be rejected.
# mapping command -> ActionExecutor
commandActionExecutors:
#  PAUSE: orderengine:50200
#  RESUME: orderengine:50200
  CANCEL: orderengine:50200
  ABORT: orderengine:50200
  TRANSFER: orderengine:50200
  default: orderengine:50200
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 621/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 622 ===
Handling
The SECS Service forwards SECS commands as SecsRawCommands.
Exception: TRANSFER, CANCEL and ABORT, if convertTransferCom-
mandsToOrderCommands: true  (see below)
1. The SECS Service builds a SecsRawCommand and sends it to the
relevant Action Executor.
The SecsRawCommand contains the remote command name and the
raw data from the S2,F41 and S2,F49 message. The raw data comes
as a byte array and as an SML string.
2. The Action Executor is then responsible for decoding the data and for
executing the command, or for returning proper error codes.
Custom remote commands will also be forwarded as SecsRawCommands
to the specified Action Executor (or the default one). They, too, are sup-
ported by the S2,F41 and S2,F49 stream function messages.
message SecsRawCommand
{
   // the command name
   string command = 1;
   // the command parameter as SML
   string data_sml = 2;
   // the command parameter as byte array
   bytes data_bytes = 3;
   // The context of this command, can be used for arbitrary, project-
specific data. 
   // Typical contexts contain a timestamp, an origin or an identifier from 
an external system. 
   // [optional]
   google.protobuf.Any context = 4;
}
TRANSFER commands can be configured to be converted to OrderCom-
mands and sent to the Action Executor that handles TRANSFER com-
mands.
By default, this Action Executor is the Order Engine.
• Configuration:
convertTransfersToOrderCommands: true
• ABORT and CANCEL will cancel the OrderCommand.
The cancel action is called on the Action Executor of the service that
is configured to handle ABORT commands or CANCEL commands.
5.17.2 Action handling
The SECS standard requires that S2,F41 and S2,F49 messages must be
acknowledged with a code.
• When an Action Executor accepts an action, it sends Code.OK (0)
to the SECS Service.
The SECS Service then forwards the SECS Response Acknowledge
Code "0" to the factory host.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
622/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 623 ===
• When an Action Executor rejects an action, it usually sends Code.IN-
VALID_ARGUMENT_VALUE to the SECS Service.
In this case, the SECS Service forwards the SECS Response Ac-
knowledge Code "2" to the factory host.
An Action Executor may give a more specific answer by sending Re-
sponseAck:, followed by the correspondig SECS Response Acknowl-
edge Code.
Example: ResponseAck:1
The Action Executor encodes its response in the message text sta-
tus.setMessage().
The SECS Service then forwards the SECS Response Acknowledge
Code it received to the factory host.
SECS Response Acknowledge Codes
Code/
integer Description
0 Acknowledgement: Command has been performed.
1 Command does not exist.
2 Cannot perform now.
3 At least one parameter is invalid.
4 Acknowledgement: Command will be performed, with com-
pletion signaled later by an event.
5 Rejected, already in desired condition
6 No such object exists.
7 … 63 Reserved
5.18 Order Engine
Description
The Order Engine is the component that schedules the tasks necessary
for carrying out orders. It is the “decision maker” that operates on the
highest level.
The Order Engine deals with what each robot should do next on a higher
level. It breaks down orders into smaller tasks and distributes these
among the robots while optimizing the tasks for throughput. Eventually, it
forwards the tasks to the Workflow Control System (WCS). The WCS is
responsible for path planning and for handling path conflicts.
Action Executor
The Order Engine has an Action Executor service for OrderCommand.
Default port: 50200.
The command is also available in the RobotManager.
// A command that submits a new order.
message OrderCommand
{
  // The order that shall be submitted. 
  oneof order {
    DisposeOrder dispose = 3;
    BufferOrder buffer = 4;
    PickOrder pick = 5;

KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 623/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 624 ===
    TransferOrder transfer = 6;
  }
  // The order's priority. The priority determines with which 
  // importance an order is processed. Priorities follow the 
  // natural ordering of integers: lower values have a higher
  // priority.
  // [optional] If not specified, the priority defaults to 0.
  google.protobuf.Int32Value priority = 1;
  // The context of an order can be used for arbitrary, 
  // project-specific data. Typical contexts contain a 
  // timestamp, an origin or an identifier from an external 
  // system. 
  // [optional]
  google.protobuf.Any context = 2;
}
 
Order Runtime Information
The Order Engine publishes runtime information for all orders as part of
the action subscription.
The message OrderRuntimeInformation is continuously updated whenever
the tasks change. For example, tasks change when the allocated robot
changes, when a task is canceled or when a state changes.
// Runtime information of the order processing.
message OrderRuntimeInformation
{
  // List of tasks that will be executed to complete the order.
  repeated Task tasks = 1;
}
// A task consists of a trip to a station and a pick or drop
// action that is executed there.
message Task

  string payload_uuid = 3;
  // The UUID of the robot that executes this task.
  string robot_uuid = 4;
  // The UUID of the robot compartment, where the payload is
  // picked or dropped.
  string robot_compartment_uuid = 5;
  // The UUID of the station, where the payload is picked
  // or dropped.
  string station_uuid = 6;
  // The UUID of the station compartment, where the payload is

  string station_compartment_uuid = 7;
  // The destination of the robot's trip, where the task action

CLI examples
For the following examples, it is assumed that kuka is an alias for dock-
er-compose -f /opt/server/docker-compose.yml run cli .
Orders can be commanded in 2 styles:
• Compact: uses the most frequently used parameters
• Verbose: all parameters can be set
Examples: compact
Submit a transfer order for payload PL0 to station S1, compartment C1:
kuka action OrderCommand --transfer "PL0,S1,C1"
Submit a transfer order for payload PL0 to station S1 and have the Order
Engine select a compartment automatically:
kuka action OrderCommand --transfer "PL0,S1"
Submit a pick order for payload PL0 with priority 2:
kuka action OrderCommand --priority 2 --pick "PL0"
Submit a dispose order for payload PL0. Optionally, you can set the sta-
tion and the compartment for disposal, otherwise they are selected auto-
matically:
kuka action OrderCommand --dispose "PL0"
kuka action OrderCommand --dispose "PL0,S2"
kuka action OrderCommand --dispose "PL0,S2,C1"
Examples: verbose
Submit a dispose order for the payload currently located at station S1,
compartment C1:
kuka action OrderCommand \
  --dispose.payload.location.station "S1,C1"
Submit a pick order for robot R1 for payload PL0:
kuka action OrderCommand \
  --pick.payload "P0" \
  --pick.robot "R1"
Submit a pick order for the payload currently located at station S1, com-
partment C1. Robot R2 is to transfer the payload to compartment C3:

kuka action OrderCommand \
  --pick.payload.location.station "S1,C1" \
  --pick.robot "R2,C3"
Submit a transfer order for the payload currently carried by robot R2 in

  --transfer.payload.location.robot "R2,C3" \

PDDL requirements
The Order Engine uses a PDDL planner to subdivide orders into tasks.
The planner supports these PDDL requirements:
:strips
:typing
:negative-preconditions
:disjunctive-preconditions
:action-costs
5.18.1 Order Engine: orders
Orders
An order is an intralogistic task. Orders are the highest-level abstraction of
the logistic operations in a plant.
Typical orders are transport requests. These can be of different scope,
e.g. the transport of a specific payload to a specific destination, or a re-
quest to buffer anything currently carried by a robot to the nearest buffer
station.
An order may require that several robots work together for it to be accom-
plished.
Some orders can be assigned to specific robots. If an order is assigned to
a robot, then the fundamental tasks of that order will be restricted to that
robot. Other robots can still be used for preparatory tasks, like clearing
the destination of a transfer.
Priorities
Orders have a priority. Priorities suggest which orders should be pro-
cessed first when the overall workload exceeds the fleet's work capacity.
• The lower the integer value of the priority, the higher the priority! Ex-
amples:
Priority 1 is higher (i.e. more important) than priority 2.
Priority 0 is higher than 1.
Priority -3 is higher than 0.
• Priorities do not (!) define a strict processing sequence:
Orders of a lower priority may start or may be finished before orders
of a higher priority.
The reason is that apart from priorities, other factors are also taken in-
to account when processing tasks, e.g. the start positions of the ro-
bots, execution delays, cross-order dependencies, optimization of the
total makespan, etc.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
626/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 627 ===
• For orders that have the same priority, usually the order that was sub-
mitted first will be processed first, although this is not a hard and fast
rule.
• Priorities have a relative and an absolute meaning.
Example: Order O1 has priority 1. Order O2 has a lower priority, but
can be completed much faster.
O2 will much more likely be processed first if its priority is 2 than if it
is 9.
Priorities are meant to only roughly group orders according to their im-
portance.
Do not try to control the order processing with priorities in great detail,
as this could actually have a negative effect on the overall performance.
Timestamps
Orders have a creation timestamp. It implies the “age” of the order.
In general, orders with older timestamps are processed first, but more re-
cent ones can precede if this reduces the overall duration.
5.18.1.1 TransferOrder
A TransferOrder transfers a payload to a specific station. Usually this hap-
pens by executing a drop task.
• Depending on whether the payload is currently placed on a station or
a robot, a pick task may be necessary first.
• When a target compartment is specified that is currently occupied by
another payload, then that payload must be picked up first also.
When the payload is already at the target station, the order finishes imme-
diately.
message TransferOrder
{
  // Specifies the payload to be transported.
  PayloadSpecification payload = 1;
  // Specifies where the payload must be dropped.
  // If the target has more than one compartment but is not
  // specified, an empty compartment is chosen automatically.
  StationSpecification target = 2;
  // Specifies the robot that must transfer the payload.
  // [optional] If not specified, a suitable robot is chosen
  //    automatically.
  // If the robot's compartment is already used by a different
  // payload, the order fails.
  RobotSpecification robot = 3;
}
5.18.1.2 PickOrder
A PickOrder transfers a payload to a robot. This usually happens by exe-
cuting a pick task.
When the payload is already on the robot, the order finishes immediately.
message PickOrder
{
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 627/719

  // Specifies the payload to be picked.
  PayloadSpecification payload = 1;
  // Specifies the robot that must pick the payload.
  // [optional] If not specified, a suitable robot is chosen
  //    automatically.
  // If the robot's compartment is already used by a different
  // payload, the order fails.
  RobotSpecification robot = 2;
}
5.18.1.3 DisposeOrder
A DisposeOrder removes a payload from the system. This happens by
dropping the payload into the disposal compartment of a station. After-
wards, the payload is deleted and KUKA.AMR Fleet is no longer account-
able for it.
Usually the payload is then picked up and processed further by external
systems, e.g. robot arms, forklift trucks or humans.
message DisposeOrder
{
  // Specifies the payload to be disposed.
  PayloadSpecification payload = 1;
  // Specifies where the payload must be disposed.
  // [optional] If not specified, a suitable dispose station is
  //    chosen automatically.
  // If the target is not a dispose station, the order fails.
  StationSpecification target = 2;
  // Specifies the robot that must transfer the payload.
  // [optional] If not specified, a suitable robot is chosen
  //    automatically.
  // If the robot's compartment is already used by a different
  // payload, the order fails.
  RobotSpecification robot = 3;
}
5.18.1.4 BufferOrder
A BufferOrder transfers a payload to a buffer compartment.
When the target buffer compartment is not specified, the nearest vacant
compartment is automatically selected.
A BufferOrder can be used to temporarily store a payload, either to keep
it ready at hand or because it cannot currently be transferred to its actual
destination.
message BufferOrder
{
  // Specifies the payload to be transported.
  PayloadSpecification payload = 1;
  // Specifies where the payload must be dropped. 
  // [optional] If not specified, a suitable buffer station is
  //    chosen automatically.
  // If the target is not a buffer station, the order fails.
  StationSpecification target = 2;
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15

  // If the robot's compartment is already used by a different

  RobotSpecification robot = 3;

5.18.2 Order Engine: optimization
Optimization
The Order Engine optimizes the distribution of tasks among the robots
and the sequencing of tasks. Aims:
• To complete individual orders as fast as possible
• To minimize the overall duration for all orders
• To maximize throughput
These aims constitute a constraint optimization problem. To solve the
problem, the Order Engine uses constraint programming techniques.
The Order Engine distinguishes between hard constraints and soft con-
straints.
Hard constraints
Hard constraint:
• A physical limit or a rule that must not be broken
Hard constraints are not allowed to be violated and constitute a constraint
satisfaction problem.
Examples:
• Robots cannot transport more payload than their total transport capaci-
ty.
• A robot cannot execute a drop task for a payload it is not carrying.
• Robots with a critical battery level must not be used for order process-
ing.
• Payloads can only be placed in compartments that accept payloads of
that specific type.
The Order Engine implements all hard constraints necessary for a safe
operation.
Soft constraints
Soft constraint:
• A guideline for what to consider when allocating tasks
Soft constraints describe the objective function that is to be maximized.
The Order Engine implements these soft constrains:
• Process orders with a higher priority before orders with a lower priori-
ty.
• Process orders with an older timestamp before orders with a more re-
cent timestamp.
• Prefer compartments with a higher priority over compartments with a
lower priority.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 629/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 630 ===
• Minimize the overall motion duration for all robots.
• Maximize the number of tasks finished per duration of time.
• For order processing, avoid robots with a low battery level.
• For order processing, avoid robots that are currently charging.
The soft constraints often conflict with each other. The Order Engine
weighs them against each other and finds reasonable trade-offs.
 
Examples
The examples illustrate the basic principle of how the Order Engine may
evaluate a scenario.
In a real application, numerous other factors will be taken into account,
depending on the specific circumstances.
 
Priorities: example 1
Scenario:
• Order O1 transfers payload P1 from source station S1 to target station
T1.
• Order O2 transfers payload P2 from source station S2 to target station
T2.
• Distance S1 -> T1 is the same as S2 -> T2.
• O2 has a higher priority than O1.
• Robot R1 has a transport capacity of 2.
• Distance R1 -> T1/S1 is the same as R1 -> S2/T2.
• Both orders take the same amount of time.
Fig. 5-670: Priorities: example 1
In example 1, the robot first processes O2, then O1.
The order in which O1 and O2 are processed has no effect on the overall
duration. The priority is the decisive factor here.
 
Priorities: example 2
Scenario:
• Orders, priorities and robot transport capacity as in example 1
• Distance R1 -> T1/S1 is considerably smaller than R1 -> S2/T2.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
630/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Programming and configuration


=== PDF PAGE 631 ===
Fig. 5-671: Priorities: example 2
In example 2, the robot first processes O1, then O2. The overall duration
is the decisive factor here.
When the robot processes O1 first, the overall duration is much shorter
than if O2 was first.
When O1 is processed first, the delay for O2 is relatively small, because
in moving from S1 to T1 the robot is already on its path to S2.
Note: The priority is taken into consideration just the same. See exam-
ple 3.
 
Priorities: example 3
Scenario:
• As in example 2, but this time with the priority of O2 considerably (!)
higher than that of O1
This scenario would again lead to O2 being processed first.
Fig. 5-672: Priorities: example 3
 
Timestamps
For timestamps, the behavior is analogous to priorities: In general, orders
with older timestamps are processed first, but more recent ones can pre-
cede if this reduces the overall duration.
 
Robot utilization: example 1
Scenario:
• Order O1 transfers payload P1 from source station S1 to target station
T1.
• Order O2 transfers payload P2 from source station S2 to target station
T2.
• Distance S1 -> T1 is the same as S2 -> T2.
• Robot R1 has a transport capacity of 2.
• Robot R2 has a transport capacity of 2.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 631/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 632 ===
• Distance R1 -> S1/T1 is the same as R2 -> S2/T2. Distance is small.
Fig. 5-673: Robot utilization: example 1
In example 1, R1 processes O1, and R2 processes O2.
If, on the other hand, R1 processed both orders, the second order would
be completed much later. The overall driving distance would also be great-
er, because of the extra route from T1 to S2.
Using only R2 would take even longer than using only R1.
 
Robot utilization: example 2
Scenario:
S1 and S2 as well as T1 and T2 are close to each other, and both robots
are in proximity of the source stations.
Fig. 5-674: Example: robot utilization (2)
In example 2, one robot (either R1 or R2) processes both O1 and O2.
If, on the other hand, the orders were processed by different robots, this
would double the overall driving distance, but without completing the or-
ders significantly sooner than with one robot only.

# Configures the expected average speed for all robots.
# The mean speed is used to estimate the duration until a robot
# arrives at a task's target node. It is affected by rotations,
# obstacle avoidance and communication with periphery, so it is
# lower than the normal speed.
# unit: meters per second [m/s]
# type: float
# range: (0; ∞)
meanRobotSpeed: 0.5
# Configures the battery threshold below which a robot is in a
# critical state. Robots with a critical battery are not used
# for order processing. Ongoing tasks are canceled.
# unit: 
# type: float
# range: [0; 1]
criticalBatteryState: 0.1
# Configures the battery threshold below which a robot is in a
# low state. Robots with a low battery that are not charging 
# are avoided to be used for new orders. They can finish their
# scheduled tasks. Robots with a low battery that are charging
# are not used until their state of charge passes this 
# threshold.
# unit:

# type: float
# range: [0; 1]
lowBatteryState: 0.25
# Configures the battery threshold until which a robot will try
# to charge. Robots with a lower battery state that are 
# charging are avoided to be used for orders.
# unit: 
# type: float
# range: [0; 1]

# Configures the expected mean base duration for task types.

# type: int
# range: [0; ∞)

# Configures the action that is executed on arrival for pick
# and drop tasks. At runtime, this template will be filled with
# detailed information about the corresponding task. The 
# sequence field will be set to 1. The actionId field will be
# set to the task uuid. The string fields param1 to param5 are
# transformed, the following substrings are replaced with the
# task parameters:
#   $station$: the uuid of the station where the action is executed
#   $payload$: the uuid of the payload that is picked or dropped
#   $source$: uuid of the compartment from which the payload is taken
#   $target$: uuid of the compartment where the payload is placed
# type: kuka.wcs.grpc.mission.ActionGto
actionOnArrived:
  pick:
    type: IISY_ARM_APPLICATION
    param1: PickApplication
    param5: >-       { "payload":"$payload$",
        "station":"$station$",
        "source":"$source$",
        "target":"$target$"}
  drop:
    type: IISY_ARM_APPLICATION
    param1: DropApplication        param5: >- 
      { "payload":"$payload$",
        "station":"$station$",
        "source":"$source$",
        "target":"$target$"}
Payloads
A payload is the smallest independently transportable unit in a scenario.
Robots can pick up payloads from storage stations, transfer them and
drop them in storage stations.
Typical payloads: item containers, racks, body shells
Payloads are persisted here: /opt/server/config/payload.res (format: JSON)
General structure:

Parameter Description
id Unique identifier of the payload
locationType “station”, “robot”, “absent”
States whether the payload is currently located on a station, a robot,
or whether it is absent.
location UUID of the station or robot with the payload
placeOnLocation Identifier of the place on the station or robot where the payload is lo-
cated
payloadType String. Payload types usually have different physical properties. Most
of the time, payloads of the same type can be exchanged, while pay-
loads of different types cannot.
state String, e.g. “FULL”, “EMPTY”, “BROKEN”, or similar
Note: state is always project-specific.
5.18.4 Order Engine: dependencies
The Order Engine has dependencies to other services in the system.
Data Providers
The Order Engine requires the Data Provider services listed here.
RobotData
Data: robots that are currently available
The Order Engine uses the robots according to their capabilities.
Robots that have been removed are no longer used, and their tasks are
allocated to other robots, if possible.
TransportCapacity
Data: the payload each robot can carry
Every robot that can transport payload must have a Data Provider for
TransportCapacity. (Obviously, the server robot must not provide any
transport capacity.)
BatteryState
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 635/719
Programming and configuration

=== PDF PAGE 636 ===
Data: charging levels of the robots, robots that have a charging level suffi-
cient for order processing
Every robot that has a battery must have a Data Provider for BatteryState.
The Order Engine allows robots with a low charging level enough time for
charging.
StationData
Data: available stations, how much payload stations can store and what
types, capabilities of the stations
The Order Engine uses the data to check where payloads can be picked
up or dropped.
GraphData
Data: locations of stations, locations of nodes
The Order Engine uses the data for estimating driving distances, for opti-
mizing the overall schedule for the fleet, and for determining which nodes
to move the robots to in order to execute a pick action or drop action.
Some orders allow for automatic station selection. In this case, the dis-
tance is relevant for identifying the nearest suitable station.
FleetData
Data: graphs and nodes where the robots are currently located
The Order Engine uses the data for its initial estimation of the driving dis-
tance from the robot's current node to the target.
Also: for tracking the travel progress and for updating the remaining time
for tasks currently being executed.
If, for example, a robot is delayed because of obstacles or motion errors,
the Order Engine registers this and may decide to re-allocate the robot's
follow-up tasks.
Payloads
The Order Engine requires information about the locactions of the pay-
loads. This is in order to know where a robot must move when a payload
must be picked up, or whether the payload is currently carried by a robot.
Payloads are initially read from:
/opt/server/config/payload.res
When a pick action or drop action finishes, the Order Engine updates the
payload location and persists the change in the same file.
WCS
The Order Engine requires the WCS gRPC service for executing pick
tasks and drop tasks.
Tasks are translated into missions that move the robot to the target node
and, on arrival, execute a pick action or drop action.
The Order Engine also subscribes to the mission status to track and pub-
lish the task states.

636/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026

6 API framework
6.1 Overview
The actual APIs are explained in the respective sections of the individual
functionalities.
The Mobile Robotics APIs are network-based. Each component hosts a
gRPC server that optionally hosts 2 services:
• Action Executor: a generic interface for commanding actions
• Data Provider: a generic interface for requesting data
All features can be categorized into one of these 2 services. Advantages:
• The same logic can be used to track the state of action executions.
• The same logic can be used to read data from Mobile Robotics serv-
ices.
• Generic data flows can be leveraged to forward robot data to the serv-
er and vice versa (for both action commands and robot data).
• The system is extensible and customizable with new services that also
implement such gRPC services.
6.2 List of Action Executors and Data Providers
The table in this section can be used as a quick reference on which serv-
ice publishes which data.
The services are available from their respective networks:
• Server services: The Docker Compose file specifies the “server” net-
work the services are available from by specifying the host and port
listed in the table.
The CLI tool on the server side is already within the server network
and can be used directly to communicate with server services.
• Fleet simulation services : Can be made available (if applicable) by
specifying the host name and port listed in the table. In order to reach
the services, the client must be within the simulated robot network, it
must be defined in the simulation's Docker Compose file and it must
be named according to this pattern: “sim-[ROBOT_UUID]”.
• Robot services  are run on the robot. They can only be accessed
from the robot host network using 127.0.0.1 as host name instead of
the host listed in the list.
In addition, the Robot Manager acts as a proxy for all services and can
also be used to collect data from any service and to command actions.
Location: server

Fleet Manager fleetmanager:50070 GraphMotionCom-
mand

mand
FleetData
Graph graph:50080 GraphData

components. This data is forwarded to the server to be generically dis-

6.3 Action Executors
A Mobile Robot is able to perform various actions, such as:
• Moving from point A to point B
• Triggering an arm application
• Detecting objects
• …
Fleet Managers also perform actions, such as commanding mobile robots
to move on a graph, collecting their diagnostic data and much more.
These actions all have something in common: they are commanded and
they have a beginning and an end. The Action Executor interface stand-
ardizes the execution of such actions.
Functions of an Action Executor:
• Provides a gRPC-based interface for accepting commands
• Informs the user about status updates regarding the command
• Allows users to update and cancel commands when it makes sense
physically
Issuing actions
There are 2 ways to issue actions:
• Direct access to the Action Executor service, offered by the module
that is able to process the commands
• Use of a proxy service like the Robot Controller (on-robot) or the Ro-
bot Manager (on-server)
Both methods provide the same gRPC interface and can command the
same actions.
Components differ with regard to the types of actions they support.
6.3.1 Requesting the types of actions supported by a service
An action executor service may be able to execute multiple types of ac-
tions, i.e. the motion module can execute Virtual Line Motion actions as
well as RelativeMotion actions.
Another example is the proxy service “Robot Manager”: It can execute all
action types provided by its managed mobile robots. When using a proxy
service, it may be useful to request a list of all action types supported by
the service.
RPC for requesting a list of action types supported by an action executor:
// Returns the Protobuf full names corresponding to the types of actions 
provided by this service.
rpc GetActionTypes(GetActionTypesRequest) returns (GetActionTypesResponse);
Request and responses:
// Request for getting all supported action types.
message GetActionTypesRequest {
    // Header containing the metadata of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
}
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 639/719
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 640 ===
// Response to send all supported action types.
message GetActionTypesResponse
{
    // Header containing the metadata of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
    // Protobuf message full names corresponding to the types of the actions.
    // This is the action type name that must be provided with an 
ActionDescription command.
    repeated string action_types = 2;
}
kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader can be ignored. It is intended to pro-
vide meta-data about the request, but it is currently not fully integrated in
the system.
6.3.2 Subscribing to action states and runtime information
When working with an action executor, users should subscribe to action
states and runtime information. All status changes and updates in the run-
time data (i.e. distance covered) are published through this subscription.
This subscription is especially relevant for tracking the execution state of
the commanded actions.
// Subscribe to state changes of actions.
// When calling this method all state changes of all actions are received 
via the subscribe action response.
rpc SubscribeActions (SubscribeActionsRequest) returns (stream 
SubscribeActionsResponse);
Request and response:
message SubscribeActionsRequest {
    // Header containing the metadata of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
}
message SubscribeActionsResponse {
    // Header containing the metadata of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
    // The set of actions.
    repeated Action actions = 2;
}
Generally, gRPC functions exposed by KUKA Mobile Robotics' software
use a Request and a Response message. This ensures API
consistency and extensibility.
The actions field in the SubscribeActionsResponse message contains the
following information:
• The command that is being executed through this action
• A state describing the action: CREATED, QUEUED, EXECUTING,
PAUSED or FINISHED.
The state also contains information about whether a cancel command
has been received for this action, as well as information about poten-
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
640/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
API framework


=== PDF PAGE 641 ===
tial execution errors or warnings. All accepted actions must at least
publish their FINISHED state once to all subscribers.
The other states may be skipped if the action type does not support
them. For example, importing a map is a one-shot action and is never
in the EXECUTING state.
• Runtime information: This includes an estimation of the duration as
well as custom data in the form of a Google protobuf Any message.
To read the custom data, it must be unpacked using the appropriate
message type.
6.3.3 Commanding actions
The following call on an action executor triggers the execution of a com-
mand:
// Submits the provided action request for execution to an action executor.
// The response of this RPC call returns the information whether the action 
executor has received
// the command and will execute it.
// Note that the response does not represent the result of the execution 
itself as the action executor
// processes the action asynchronously. The execution state of an action 
(e.g. if the action has been
// executed successfully) can be checked using the RPC call SubscribeActions.
// If the call fails (i.e., the returned response contains a status code), 
then it is
// ensured that this action won't be executed.
rpc SubmitAction (SubmitActionRequest) returns (SubmitActionResponse);
Action Description
The request message contains an action description:
message ActionDescription {
    // The uuid of this action.
    string uuid = 1;
    // The uuid of the resource to execute this action.
    string resource_uuid = 2;
    // The timestamp corresponding to the time of the creation of this 
action description.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp timestamp = 3;
    // The action command with all its parameters.
    google.protobuf.Any command = 4;
}
The command is stored in a Google protobuf Any field to ensure the ge-
nericity of the interface.
The command type will be used by the action executor module to match
the action with a corresponding action implementation. For example, the
motion module in the Navigation system can deal with command messag-
es of the type: kuka.mobilerobotics.motion.v1alpha1.VirtualLineMotionCom-
mand in order to execute this kind of motion.
Proxy services also use the type name of the command message to for-
ward the actions to a capable service.
In addition, the command contains generic action parameters:
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 641/719
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 642 ===
• A uuid field that must be unique. Actions with ambiguous UUIDs will
be rejected by Action Executor services.
• The resource_uuid field represents the Mobile Robot that is to execute
the action. The proxy services Robot Controller and Robot Manager
use this field to forward the action commands to the corresponding ro-
bots.
• A timestamp for diagnostics and logging
SubmitActionResponse
The SubmitActionResponse message contains the direct feedback about
the command:
If an error occurs while the action is being commanded, it is provided
through this response and the action will be rejected.
If there is no error, the action states and runtime data are available
through the SubscribeActions mechanism.
6.3.4 Updating and canceling actions
Not all action executors support the updating and canceling of actions.
However, use cases for updating or canceling do exist:
• The target of a graph motion changed (update).
• An application is canceled for any reason.
For such use cases, action executors expose the following RPCs:
// Updates an action.
// Note that the response does not represent the result of the update itself 
as the action executor
// processes the request asynchronously. The state of an action (e.g. if the 
action has been
// updated successfully) can be checked using the RPC call SubscribeActions.
// If the call fails (i.e., the returned response contains a status code), 
then it is
// ensured that this action won't be updated.
rpc UpdateAction (UpdateActionRequest) returns (UpdateActionResponse);
// Cancels the specified actions.
// This operation stops the execution of the given actions.
// All actions depending on the canceled actions are automatically canceled.
// The cancellation of referenced actions that do not exist is ignored.
// Note that the response does not represent the result of the cancellation 
itself as the action executor
// processes the request asynchronously. The state of an action (e.g. if the 
action has been
// cancelled successfully) can be checked using the RPC call SubscribeActions.
rpc CancelActions (CancelActionsRequest) returns (CancelActionsResponse);
Once CancelActions is called, a cancelation flag is set to TRUE on the
action states of the corresponding actions.
The flag can be used to determine whether an action was canceled.
An action that has been canceled is not necessarily in the FINISHED
state: clean-up operations or braking of the robots may still be pending for
some time.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
642/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 643 ===
6.3.5 Generic Action Executor
Description
The Generic Action Executor can be used as a stub for simulating hard-
ware actions and for testing whether a workflow is operational.
When the Generic Action Executor receives an action with a specified
message type, it executes the file with the name that corresponds to the
name of the message type.
• An action that has been carried out and is finished is in the FINISHED
state. This also applies if an error occurred.
• The result of the exit code is used to set error_state in the action re-
sponse: In case of an error, error_state contains information about it.
Updating and canceling of actions is not supported.
Precondition
• The specified message types are available.
• The specified message types are suitable for the specific service.
Procedure
1. Create a script: Name it after the desired message and add the ".sh"
postscript.
Example:
/opt/server/action_scripts/kuka.mobilerobotics.appli-
cationcontrol.ExecuteApplicationAction.sh
The script must exit with 0 if it succeeds and with <> 0 if it fails.
Example:
#!/bin/bash
echo "Hello World"
exit 0
2. Make your script executable.
Example:
chmod a+x /opt/server/action_scripts/kuka.mobilero-
botics.applicationcontrol.ExecuteApplicationAction.sh
3. Add the configuration to /opt/server/docker-compose.yml.
simulateactionexecutor:
  image: "${REGISTRY_PATH}/nav-genericactionexecutor:$
{TAG}"
  container_name: simulateactionexecutor
  restart: always
  volumes:
    - /opt/server/action_scripts:/opt/robot/scripts
  environment:
     GRPC_SERVER_PORT: 50230
  logging:
    options:
      max-size: "10m"
      max-file: "10"
Please keep in mind that the required packages may not yet have
been installed in the container. Consider writing your own docker file
with a dependency to the missing packages.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 643/719
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 644 ===
4. Add the service to config/robot-controller-config.yml.
6.4 Data Providers
Robots collect data from their sensors. Robots generate data from their
runtime or by executing algorithms that produce data. In order to enable
integrators to write efficient applications, it is important that robot data is
available and easily accessible. For this reason, the Data Provider con-
cept is one of the basic principles of the API of the mobile robots.
Functions of a Data Provider:
• Provides a gRPC-based interface for accessing data
• Exposes a subscription mechanism for receiving data streams
• Exposes a getter interface for reading data in a one-shot process
Accessing data
There are 2 ways to read data:
• Direct access to the service that generates the data
• Use of a proxy service like the Robot Controller (on-robot) or the Ro-
bot Manager (on-server)
Both methods provide the same gRPC interface and can receive the
same data.
6.4.1 Requesting the types of data provided by a service
A data provider service may provide multiple types of data, i.e. the laser
module can provide “LaserData” as well as “LaserDiagnosticData”.
Another example is the proxy service “Robot Manager”: It can provide all
data types provided by its managed mobile robots. When using a proxy
service, it may be useful to request a list of all data types provided by the
service.
RPC for requesting a list of data types exposed by a data provider:
// Returns the Protobuf URI corresponding to the types of data provided by 
this service.
rpc GetDataTypes(GetDataTypesRequest) returns (GetDataTypesResponse);
Request and responses:
message GetDataTypesRequest
{
    // Header containing information about the origin of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
}
message GetDataTypesResponse
{
    // Header containing information about the origin of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
    // Protobuf message URI corresponding to the type of the requested data.
    // This is the data type name that must be provided with a 
SubscribeDataRequest or GetDataRequest.
    repeated string data_types = 2;
}
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
644/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 645 ===
kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader can be ignored. It is intended to pro-
vide meta-data about the request, but it is currently not fully integrated in
the system.
6.4.2 Subscribing to data
RPC for providing data of a specific type:
// Starts a stream which provides data at a configured frequency.
rpc SubscribeData(SubscribeDataRequest) returns (stream SubscribeDataResponse);
Request and response messages for getting the GetDataTypes call:
message SubscribeDataRequest
{
    // Header containing information about the origin of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
    // Protobuf message URI corresponding to the type of data being 
requested.
    // The data type must match with the type returned by "GetDataType".
    string data_type = 2;
    // The requested frequency of the data subscription (in Hertz).
    // If set to zero, data shall be published whenever it changes
    // Otherwise, the service shall provide the data with a frequency as 
close to the requested value
    // as possible.
    double frequency = 3;
    
    // Optional filters reducing the scope of the request.
    // Please refer to the documentation of the component being accessed for 
a list of supported filters.
    repeated google.protobuf.Any filters = 4;
}
message SubscribeDataResponse
{
    // Header containing information about the origin of this message.
    kuka.core.v1.MessageHeader header = 1;
    // The requested data.
    google.protobuf.Any data = 2;
}
Generally, gRPC functions exposed by KUKA Mobile Robotics' software
use a Request and a Response message. This ensures API
consistency and extensibility.
Depending on the filters and the frequency, the response is received in a
data stream. The response contains the data, packaged in a google.proto-
buf.Any message.
Unpackaging the data must be done on the client side.
Setting the frequency
The frequency (Hertz) for the subscription can be set in the SubscribeDa-
taRequest message, in order to limit the amount of network traffic caused
by the data exchange. Each service defines a maximum frequency.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 645/719
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 646 ===
Set the frequency to “0” in order to receive updates only when there is
new data.
Filtering data
Some components accept a filter to limit the amount of data being trans-
ferred. For example, the map service provides a filter for map UUIDs.
With this filter, only data related to the map with the specified UUID will
be transferred.
Filters are component specific.
6.4.3 Reading data (one-shot)
Sometimes it is not desirable to open a subscription stream because data
is required only once. For example, reading a laser snapshot (location) is
done in one-shot style whenever a fine localization action is executed.
RPC:
// Returns the latest data managed by the data provider.
rpc GetData(GetDataRequest) returns (GetDataResponse);
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
646/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
API framework

=== PDF PAGE 647 ===

for details on
the method to

cessToken.

code.

options must be fil-

Code. The node is

er code.

UUID of

KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 659/719
KUKA-interface standard API

=== PDF PAGE 660 ===
Name Type Max
Length
Re-
quired
Default
Value Description
cancelMode String T FORCE Cancel Mode:
• FORCE: Force cancel
Immediately terminate the
current mission.
• NORMAL: Normal cancel
Does not cancel the task
that the robot is execut-
ing, and waits for the ro-
bot to complete the cur-
rent task before canceling
the workflow.
• REDIRECT_END: Redi-
rect to the end node of
the workflow.
Does not cancel the task
that the robot is execut-
ing, and waits for the ro-
bot to complete the cur-
rent task. If the target
node does not require
lowering the rack, the ro-
bot carries the rack to the
end node; otherwise, the
empty robot moves to the
end node.
• REDIRECT_START: Redi-
rect to the start node of
the workflow.
Does not cancel the task
that the robot is execut-
ing, and waits for the ro-
bot to complete the cur-
rent task. If the target
node does not require
lowering the rack, the ro-
bot carries the rack back
to the start node; other-
wise, the empty robot
moves back to the start
node.
Currently, only tasks for lift ro-
bots support all strategies,
while other types of robots
support FORCE only. Howev-
er, it still depends on whether
the robot supports cancella-
tion.
reason String F Reason to cancel.
Request body example
{
  "requestId": "request202309250006",
  "missionCode": "mission202309250004",

  "position": "",
  "cancelMode": "FORCE",
  "reason": ""
}

of the running

API Name missionStateCallback

• ROLLER_SEND
• PICKER_RECEIVE
• PICKER_SEND
• FORK_UP
• FORK_DOWN

• COMPLETED
• CANCELED
message String F Description message.

needs to be reported.

payloadPoseY Double Payload pose of the robot - Y.
payloadPoseTheta Double Payload pose of the robot - Theta.
payloadCheckCode String Payload validation code; QR code number.
errorMessage String Robot error message.
It consists of an error code and error informa-
tion. This field has a value only when the ro-
bot is in error.

    "code": "100001",
    "message": "only one of area ID or area code can 
be filled in",
    "success": false
}

  "message": "", //Error description
  "success": false,
  "data": null
}

unacceptable.

ing.

the allowed options.
No such node. 100204 The requested node does not
exist.

type does not exist.

8 Diagnosis
8.1 Downloading diagnostic data (ZIP file)
Diagnostics Service
The Diagnostics Service is the robot navigation system's service in charge
of collecting data relevant for diagnostic purposes. It packages the data in
a single ZIP file.
The Diagnostics Service provides an Action Executor interface for starting
and configuring the process.
TCP port:
By default, the gRPC services offered by the Diagnostics Service can be
reached on TCP port 50140.
Action Executor interface
kuka.actionexecutor.v1alpha1.ActionExecutorService offers the SubmitAc-
tion RPC method.
The actions field of the SubmitActionRequest must to be set to an in-
stance of:
kuka.actionexecutor.v1alpha1.ActionDescription
params must contain an instance of:
kuka.diagnostics.v1alpha1.CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand
// The command to request the creation of a diagnostics package (zip file 
containing e.g. log files) from the diagnostics component.
message CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand
{
  // the target URI or file path where the resulting diagnostics package 
will be stored.
  // might be either a directory or the resulting file path.
  // e.g. "/output/diagnostics/", "file:///output/diagnostics/", or "file:///
output/diagnostics/diagnostics.zip"
  // if not given, the defaultPackageDestinationFolder value of the 
"diagnostics-config.yml" configuration file is used.
  // rsync:
  //        additionally, 'rsync' is a supported scheme, which will invoke 
rsync to deliver the resulting diagnostics package to a network host.
  //        e.g. "rsync://host:port/module/path" will be directly passed to 
rsync as destination parameter. see rsync documentation (https://
download.samba.org/pub/rsync/rsync.1) for more details.
  //        this rsync scheme supports all destination parameter variations 
of rsync. in general, the leading "rsync:" is cut and not passed as part of 
the destination parameter to rsync. 
  //        the only exception is the "rsync://" daemon mode, where this 
rule is not applied.
  //        also supported rsync scheme variations: "rsync:host::module/
path" (daemon mode), "rsync:host:path" (remote shell (ssh) mode)
  //        the rsync scheme also supports a URI query, and all query parts 
will be passed to rsync as additional parameters.
  //        e.g. "rsync://host:port/module/path?--mkpath&--port=10873" will 
pass "--mkpath" and "--port=10873" to rsync.
  //        for convenience, if host is omitted, the IP address of the 
CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand issuer will be used, so the requester does 
not need to know its public IP address,
  //        but this is only useful if the CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand 
is directly sent to the diagnostics service, otherwise the delivery will 
most likely fail (e.g. if sent via a robot-controller/manager).
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 709/719
Diagnosis

=== PDF PAGE 710 ===
  //        since the network transfer could fail for many reasons, when 
the rsync scheme is used, the resulting diagnostics package will always be 
kept in the default output directory of the service's host.
  string package_destination = 1;
  // a map of supported diagnostics package content and whether it shall be 
included.
  // options that are given here will overwrite the default configuration, 
for options that are not given, their default configuration determines 
whether they will be included.
  // the string, a simple regular expression, is matched against the name, 
the srcPath and the destPath of an entry of the "diagnostics-config.yml" 
configuration file. 
  // this matching supports the asterisk '*' that matches any string. 
  // e.g. "db://*" will match any string, that starts with "db://", most 
probably only the srcPaths contains such, at least in the default 
diagnostics config file.
  // e.g. "logs/" will exactly match all entries "logs/", probably only used 
as destPath 
  map<string, bool> options = 2;
  // if > 0, this amount of seconds is used to exclude all files with an 
older modification time.
  // if <= 0, all files will be included.
  int64 time_filter_seconds = 3;
  // if the diagnostics service is configured to include diagnostics 
packages from other diagnostics services,
  // this field is used to restrict the included diagnostics packages to 
those given here.
  // this field should contain all resource UUIDs (robot UUIDs) of which a 
diagnostics package shall be included.
  // if empty, or not set, the diagnostics package of each available 
resource (robot) will be included.
  // for simplified usage, the given entries are matched against the robot 
UUIDs. 
  // matching starts at the beginning of the resource UUID and is not case-
sensitive. 
  // for example: the given filter string 'rob' will match any resource 
UUIDs starting with 'rob', 'ROB', 'Rob', and so on...
  repeated string uuid_filter = 4;
}
All fields are optional. If set, they overwrite the default values.
• package_destination: A different target folder or file may be specified
via this field. The default, /output, will be prepended, if a relative
path is specified.
• options: Defines what is packaged, e.g. folder log, a database, …
• time_filter_seconds: Via this field, outdated log files can be filtered and
will not be packaged. When the filter is set, the package will only con-
tain files that have been modified within the specified and recent time
span.
Action Executor runtime information
During command execution, an Action Executor may sporadically provide
runtime information. During execution of CreateDiagnosticsPackageCom-
mand, instances of com.kuka.common.v1alpha1.ProgressInfo are sent
whenever the execution makes progress (e.g. from 0 % to 1 %).
The amount of runtime information instances that are being received may
depend on some gRPC restrictions.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
710/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Diagnosis

=== PDF PAGE 711 ===
The creation process is made up of multiple actions. The number of ac-
tions will vary depending on the configuration, and each action is
executed individually.
CLI examples
These examples can be directed to the Robot Controller/Robot Manager
or directly to the diagnostics container action executor.
Example 1:
Create a diagnostics package in the (default) folder /output/, with con-
tent as configured in the diagnostics configuration file /opt/robot/
config/diagnostics-config.yml.
kuka action CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand -- package_destination /output/
Example 2:
Create a diagnostics package in the (default) folder /output/, with con-
tent as configured in /opt/robot/config/diagnostics-config.yml,
but restrict it to log files 1 day old at most (= 24 * 60 * 60 s = 86 400 s).
kuka action CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand --time_filter_seconds 86400
Example 3:
Create a diagnostics package in the (default) folder /output/, with con-
tent as configured in /opt/robot/config/diagnostics-config.yml,
except for the database dump of graphdb.
value:false excludes the database dump of graphdb.
kuka action CreateDiagnosticsPackageCommand --options "[{key:\"graphdb\", 
value:false}]"
8.2 Diagnostics package of KUKA.AMR Fleet
Description
Diagnostics package includes logs, databases and configuration of
KUKA.AMR Fleet. The DiagnosticsService collects all relevant diag-
nostics data and provides it as a single zip file. User can get diagnostics
package in a time range.
Precondition
• Only user role administrator can handle diagnostics operation.
Creating and downloading diagnostics package
1. Login KUKA.AMR Fleet with administrator account.
2. Go to System > Diagnostics.
Fig. 8-1: Diagnostics package interface
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 711/719
Diagnosis

=== PDF PAGE 712 ===
3. Click the Create Diagnostics Package  button.
4. Select options including:
• Age of diagnostics : Time limitation of diagnostics package. User
can select the time before now or specify the time range in the
past.
To avoid making diagnostics package too huge, the maximum
time range for a diagnostics package is 3 months. So if
selecting more than 3 months, the start time will be overwritten
into 3 months before end time.
• Quick Selection: A quick way to select the files including in the
diagnostics package. Fleet means that the files related to robot
with iiQKA.Mobility is excluded.
• Include in diagnostics package:
‒ Server logs  are all the log files in server. Default path
is /opt/server/logs.
‒ Databases of payload, map, station, location, object model are
only for robot with KUKA.Navigation OS.
‒ Databases of basic-data, fleet-dmp, wcs, ecs, krcs-simulator,
k-interface are no limited for specific AMR.
‒ Robot Diagnostics  is only for robot with KUKA.Navigation OS.
‒ Telemetry data includes short-term and long-term statistics of
server resource, like CPU, memory, I/O. It needs development
tools to analyze.
Fig. 8-2: Selecting options
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
712/719 | www.kuka.com KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026
Diagnosis


=== PDF PAGE 713 ===
Fig. 8-3: Selecting the time range
5. Click OK to start creating diagnostics package. The name starts with
"diagPackage-". When creating, other user cannot create.
Fig. 8-4: Package generating progress
6. The state changes to FINISHED when it finishes. All the diagnostics
locates in server, default path is /opt/server/diagnostics.
7. Click Download to download the diagnostics package to the PC of
current browser.
Canceling generating package
When a diagnostics package's state is EXECUTING, it can be canceled.
Click the Cancel button of the diagnostics package to stop creating and
no diagnostics package will be created.
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15
KUKA.AMR Fleet 2.15 V2 | Issued: 09.04.2026 www.kuka.com | 713/719
Diagnosis

=== PDF PAGE 714 ===
Fig. 8-5: Canceling generating package
Deleting a diagnostics package
• Manually Delete: Click Delete to delete one specific diagnostics pack-
age.
• Auto Delete: Auto deleting diagnostics is supported. The default time
to keep the diagnostics is 3 days. It can be configured in config file
diagnostics-config.yml. The default path is /opt/server/
config/diagnostics-config.yml. The parameter is as below:
packageLifespan_days: 3

9 KUKA Customer Service
Contact
This documentation provides information on operation and control and
supports you during troubleshooting. For further support, please contact
your local subsidiary.
The contact details of the local subsidiaries can be found at:
www.kuka.com/customer-service-contacts
Request
The following information is required for processing a support request:
• Description of the problem including information on the duration and
frequency of the fault
• As comprehensive information as possible about the hardware and
software components of the overall system
The following list gives an indication of the information which is rele-
vant in many cases:
‒ Model and serial number of the kinematic system, e.g. the manip-
ulator
‒ Model and serial number of the controller
‒ Model and serial number of the energy supply system
‒ Designation and version of the system software
‒ Designations and versions of other software components or modifi-
cations
‒ System software diagnosis package
Additionally for KUKA Sunrise: Existing projects including applica-
tions
For versions of KUKA System Software older than V8: Archive of
the software (Diagnosis package is not yet available here.)
‒ Application used
‒ External axes used
